<a id='question-083'></a>

### Question 083: DiGeorge Syndrome: Failure of 3rd and 4th Pharyngeal Pouches
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome) results from the failure of development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches, leading to thymic aplasia/hypoplasia (T-cell deficiency), parathyroid hypoplasia/aplasia (hypocalcemia), and conotruncal cardiac defects (e.g., Tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch). The question tests the anatomical derivatives of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches. (DiGeorge综合征 (22q11.2缺失综合征) 是由于第三和第四咽囊发育失败造成的，导致胸腺发育不全/无发育 (T细胞缺陷)、甲状旁腺发育不全/无发育 (低钙血症) 和锥干型心脏缺陷 (如四联症、主动脉弓中断)。本题考察第三和第四咽囊的解剖学衍生物。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant presents with recurrent respiratory infections, hypocalcemic seizures, and cyanosis. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died in infancy due to congenital heart disease. Physical examination reveals a systolic ejection murmur at the left upper sternal border, facial dysmorphism (short philtrum, low-set ears, hypertelorism), and tetany. Laboratory tests show low serum calcium levels and lymphopenia. Echocardiogram reveals Tetralogy of Fallot with an interrupted aortic arch type B. Karyotyping confirms a 22q11.2 deletion. Which of the following structures is most likely underdeveloped or absent in this patient due to the underlying genetic defect?

(A) Superior parathyroid gland
(B) Inferior parathyroid gland
(C) Thymus
(D) Thyroid gland
(E) Ultimobranchial body

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent respiratory infections, lymphopenia:** Suggests T-cell deficiency, pointing towards thymic hypoplasia/aplasia.
- **Hypocalcemic seizures, low serum calcium:** Suggests parathyroid hypofunction, pointing towards parathyroid hypoplasia/aplasia.
- **Cyanosis, Tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch:** Suggests conotruncal cardiac defects.
- **Facial dysmorphism:** Common feature of DiGeorge syndrome.
- **22q11.2 deletion:** Confirms the diagnosis of DiGeorge syndrome.
- **The question asks which structure is underdeveloped/absent:** This requires knowledge of the structures derived from the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches, which are affected in DiGeorge syndrome.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **DiGeorge Syndrome:** Caused by a deletion on chromosome 22q11.2, affecting the development of structures derived from the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches.
- **3rd Pharyngeal Pouch:** Gives rise to the thymus and the inferior parathyroid glands.
- **4th Pharyngeal Pouch:** Gives rise to the superior parathyroid glands and the ultimobranchial body (which contributes C-cells to the thyroid gland).
- **Clinical Manifestations:**
    - **Thymic aplasia/hypoplasia:** Leads to T-cell deficiency and recurrent infections.
    - **Parathyroid hypoplasia/aplasia:** Leads to hypocalcemia (seizures, tetany). The inferior parathyroids (from pouch 3) are typically more affected than the superior parathyroids (from pouch 4).
    - **Conotruncal cardiac defects:** Abnormal development of the aortic arches and outflow tracts.
    - **Facial dysmorphism:** Characteristic features.
- **Therefore, the thymus (derived from the 3rd pouch) is a key structure affected and is typically underdeveloped or absent in DiGeorge syndrome.** The parathyroids (both superior and inferior) and ultimobranchial body (from the 4th pouch) are also affected, but the thymus is a major derivative of the 3rd pouch and its absence explains the immunodeficiency. The thyroid gland itself develops from the pharyngeal floor and median thyroid diverticulum, not the pouches.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior parathyroid gland:** Derived from the 4th pharyngeal pouch. While often affected in DiGeorge syndrome, the inferior parathyroids (from the 3rd pouch) are usually more severely impacted. The thymus (from the 3rd pouch) is a more consistently and severely affected structure explaining the immunodeficiency.
- (B) **Inferior parathyroid gland:** Derived from the 3rd pharyngeal pouch. These are typically affected, leading to hypocalcemia. However, the thymus, also derived from the 3rd pouch, is equally or more significantly affected, causing the characteristic immunodeficiency. The question asks for *a* structure affected, and the thymus is a primary derivative of the 3rd pouch.
- (D) **Thyroid gland:** Develops from the median thyroid diverticulum (endoderm of the floor of the pharynx) and lateral thyroid diverticula (endoderm of the 4th pharyngeal pouches, forming the parafollicular C-cells). The main body of the thyroid gland is not derived from the 3rd or 4th pouches and is typically normal in DiGeorge syndrome.
- (E) **Ultimobranchial body:** Derived from the 4th pharyngeal pouch. It contributes parafollicular C-cells to the thyroid gland. While potentially affected, the thymus (from the 3rd pouch) and parathyroids (from both 3rd and 4th pouches) are more central to the classic DiGeorge phenotype.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Embryological Origin | Affected in DiGeorge Syndrome? | Clinical Consequence                                  |
| :------------------------ | :------------------- | :---------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| Thymus                    | 3rd Pharyngeal Pouch | Yes                          | T-cell deficiency, recurrent infections               |
| Inferior Parathyroid Gland | 3rd Pharyngeal Pouch | Yes                          | Hypocalcemia, seizures, tetany                        |
| Superior Parathyroid Gland | 4th Pharyngeal Pouch | Yes (often less severe)       | Hypocalcemia (less common/severe than inferior)       |
| Ultimobranchial Body      | 4th Pharyngeal Pouch | Yes                          | Potential C-cell dysfunction (less clinically prominent) |
| Thyroid Gland (main body) | Median diverticulum  | No                           | Normal function                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is characterized by the failure of development of structures derived from the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches. Key affected structures include the thymus (leading to immunodeficiency) and the parathyroid glands (leading to hypocalcemia). Remember the pouch origins: 3rd pouch -> Thymus, Inferior Parathyroid; 4th pouch -> Superior Parathyroid, Ultimobranchial body. Differentiating DiGeorge from other causes of hypocalcemia (e.g., hypoparathyroidism from surgery or autoimmune disease) or immunodeficiency is crucial.

---

<a id='question-084'></a>

### Question 084: Thyroid Surgery Complication
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anatomical relationship between the superior thyroid artery (a branch of the external carotid artery) and the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve (EBSLN) during thyroid surgery, specifically the risk of injury to the EBSLN during ligation of the superior thyroid artery. (甲状上动脉与甲状上神经外支的关系及甲状腺手术中的损伤风险)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female undergoes a total thyroidectomy for multinodular goiter. During the procedure, the surgeon meticulously dissects the superior pole of the thyroid gland. After identifying the superior thyroid artery supplying the superior pole, the surgeon ligates it close to its origin from the external carotid artery. Postoperatively, the patient reports significant difficulty projecting her voice and complains of vocal fatigue, particularly when speaking for extended periods. Examination reveals a hoarse, breathy voice, especially noticeable when attempting to shout or sing high notes. Stroboscopy confirms vocal fold immobility on the ipsilateral side of the surgery.

Which structure is most likely injured during the ligation of the superior thyroid artery in this patient?

(A) Internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(B) Recurrent laryngeal nerve
(C) Cricothyroid muscle
(D) Superior thyroid artery itself
(E) External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old female undergoing total thyroidectomy. This sets the context for potential surgical complications related to thyroid anatomy.
- **Surgical Procedure:** Ligation of the superior thyroid artery close to its origin from the external carotid artery. This is the specific maneuver implicated in the injury.
- **Postoperative Symptoms:** Difficulty projecting voice, vocal fatigue, hoarse/breathy voice, especially when shouting or singing high notes. These symptoms point towards dysfunction of the cricothyroid muscle, which is responsible for tensing the vocal cords and increasing pitch.
- **Examination Findings:** Vocal fold immobility on the ipsilateral side. This confirms nerve injury affecting the cricothyroid muscle.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure most likely injured during the ligation of the superior thyroid artery.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Superior Thyroid Artery (STA):** Arises from the external carotid artery, typically just below the level of the cartilaginous junction between the external and internal carotid arteries. It descends superiorly and medially, passing deep to the sternohyoid muscle and the omohyoid muscle belly, crossing anterior to the internal jugular vein, and then running between the thyroid cartilage and the hyoid bone to supply the superior pole of the thyroid gland.
- **External Branch of the Superior Laryngeal Nerve (EBSLN):** A branch of the superior laryngeal nerve (itself a branch of the vagus nerve, CN X). The EBSLN descends in the neck, typically running *alongside* or *deep* to the superior thyroid artery, especially in its course towards the superior pole of the thyroid. It pierces the thyrohyoid membrane and innervates the cricothyroid muscle.
- **Relationship:** The EBSLN is intimately related to the STA. During thyroid surgery, particularly when ligating the STA close to its origin or as it courses towards the superior pole, the EBSLN is at significant risk of injury due to its close proximity.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the EBSLN results in paralysis of the cricothyroid muscle. This muscle tenses the vocal cords, raising pitch. Paralysis leads to an inability to tense the vocal cords, resulting in a low-pitched, breathy voice, vocal fatigue, and difficulty projecting the voice or singing high notes. This matches the patient's postoperative symptoms.
- **Other Structures:**
    - **Internal Branch of the Superior Laryngeal Nerve (IBSLN):** Provides sensory innervation to the larynx above the vocal folds. Injury causes loss of sensation and potentially aspiration, but not the specific vocal changes described. It typically runs with the superior thyroid artery but is more medial.
    - **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN):** Innervates all intrinsic laryngeal muscles *except* the cricothyroid. Injury causes vocal cord paralysis, leading to hoarseness, breathiness, and potentially airway compromise, but typically does not affect pitch control in the same way as EBSLN injury. It runs in the tracheoesophageal groove.
    - **Cricothyroid Muscle:** The muscle itself is not injured by ligation of the artery; it is paralyzed due to nerve injury.
    - **Superior Thyroid Artery itself:** Ligation is the intended surgical step, not an injury.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The superior laryngeal nerve (and its branches) arises from the vagus nerve (CN X), which originates from the neural crest cells migrating into the pharyngeal arches. The superior thyroid artery arises from the external carotid artery, which develops from the dorsal aorta and the 3rd pharyngeal arch artery. The close anatomical relationship is established during development as these structures course through the neck region.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve:** This nerve provides sensory innervation to the larynx above the vocal cords. Injury would cause loss of sensation, potentially leading to aspiration or a cough reflex, but not the specific symptoms of vocal fatigue, difficulty projecting the voice, and inability to raise pitch described in the vignette. The IBSLN typically runs slightly more medial to the STA than the EBSLN.
- (B) **Recurrent laryngeal nerve:** This nerve innervates the posterior cricoarytenoid, lateral cricoarytenoid, interarytenoid, and thyroarytenoid muscles. Injury causes paralysis of the vocal cord, leading to hoarseness, breathiness, and potential airway compromise. While hoarseness is present, the specific symptoms of difficulty projecting the voice and inability to sing high notes are characteristic of cricothyroid muscle paralysis (EBSLN injury), not RLN injury. The RLN is located much lower in the neck, in the tracheoesophageal groove, and is typically at risk during dissection of the inferior thyroid pole or ligation of the inferior thyroid artery.
- (C) **Cricothyroid muscle:** This is the muscle that is *affected* by the nerve injury, not the structure injured by the surgical maneuver. The muscle itself is not directly damaged by ligating the artery.
- (D) **Superior thyroid artery itself:** The ligation of the superior thyroid artery is the *intended* surgical step performed by the surgeon to control bleeding from the superior pole of the thyroid gland. It is not an injury in this context, but rather the cause of the potential injury to a nearby structure.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Innervation/Function                                  | Location Relative to STA                                  | Clinical Manifestation of Injury                                  | Risk during Thyroidectomy                                  |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- |
| **EBSLN (Correct)**          | Motor to Cricothyroid muscle                          | Runs alongside/deep to STA, especially near superior pole | Inability to tense vocal cords, low pitch, vocal fatigue, breathy voice | High risk during ligation of STA near its origin/superior pole |
| IBSLN                        | Sensory to larynx above vocal cords                     | Runs alongside/deep to STA, slightly more medial than EBSLN | Loss of sensation, potential aspiration                         | Lower risk than EBSLN, but possible during superior pole dissection |
| RLN                          | Motor to all intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except CT) | Runs in tracheoesophageal groove, inferior pole region     | Vocal cord paralysis, hoarseness, breathiness, airway issues   | High risk during inferior pole dissection, ligation of inferior thyroid artery |
| Cricothyroid Muscle          | Tenses vocal cords, raises pitch                       | Anterior larynx, innervated by EBSLN                       | N/A (muscle itself)                                            | N/A (affected by nerve injury)                               |
| Superior Thyroid Artery (STA) | Blood supply to superior thyroid pole                  | Arises from ECA, runs towards superior thyroid pole        | N/A (artery itself)                                            | Ligation is the surgical step, risk to nearby EBSLN         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The close anatomical relationship between the superior thyroid artery and the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve makes the EBSLN vulnerable during thyroid surgery, particularly when ligating the STA near its origin or as it courses towards the superior pole. Injury to the EBSLN causes cricothyroid muscle paralysis, leading to a characteristic low-pitched, breathy voice and vocal fatigue. Differentiating EBSLN injury from RLN injury is crucial, as RLN injury affects all intrinsic laryngeal muscles except the cricothyroid, causing vocal cord paralysis. Remember the EBSLN innervates the cricothyroid muscle, responsible for pitch control.

<a id='question-085'></a>

### Question 085: Thyroid Surgery Complication: Vocal Cord Paralysis
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Injury to the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve (EBSLN) during thyroidectomy results in paralysis of the cricothyroid muscle, leading to an inability to produce high-pitched sounds (cricothyroid paralysis). (甲状上神经外支损伤导致甲状软骨间歇肌麻痹，无法发出高音 - 甲状软骨间歇肌麻痹)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female undergoes a total thyroidectomy for papillary thyroid carcinoma. Intraoperatively, the surgeon notes significant adhesions between the thyroid gland and the surrounding strap muscles, requiring careful dissection. Postoperatively, the patient reports a change in her voice, specifically difficulty singing high notes and producing a loud, high-pitched voice. Examination reveals normal vocal cord abduction and adduction, but the patient cannot sustain high-frequency phonation. Stroboscopy confirms medialization of the right vocal cord during attempted high-pitched phonation, consistent with cricothyroid muscle paralysis. Which nerve branch is most likely injured during the surgery, leading to this specific vocal deficit?

(A) Internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(B) External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(C) Recurrent laryngeal nerve
(D) Ansa cervicalis
(E) Phrenic nerve

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old female undergoing total thyroidectomy. This sets the context for potential surgical complications involving structures near the thyroid gland.
- **Procedure:** Total thyroidectomy. This procedure involves dissection around the thyroid gland, placing nearby nerves at risk.
- **Intraoperative Finding:** Significant adhesions. Adhesions increase the difficulty of dissection and the risk of nerve injury.
- **Postoperative Symptom:** Difficulty singing high notes, producing a loud, high-pitched voice. This is the key symptom pointing towards a specific muscle paralysis.
- **Examination Finding:** Normal vocal cord abduction/adduction, but inability to sustain high-frequency phonation. Normal abduction/adduction implicates the recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) being intact (as it controls most intrinsic laryngeal muscles including the abductor posterior cricoarytenoid and adductor thyroarytenoid/lateral cricoarytenoid/interarytenoid). The inability to produce high-pitched sounds specifically points to the cricothyroid muscle.
- **Stroboscopy:** Confirms medialization of the right vocal cord during attempted high-pitched phonation, consistent with cricothyroid muscle paralysis. This confirms the clinical suspicion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which nerve branch injury leads to this specific vocal deficit.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cricothyroid Muscle:** This muscle tenses the vocal cords, increasing pitch. It is the only intrinsic laryngeal muscle innervated by the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve (EBSLN).
- **EBSLN Course:** The EBSLN arises from the superior laryngeal nerve (itself a branch of the vagus nerve, CN X). It descends alongside the superior thyroid artery, pierces the thyrohyoid membrane, and innervates the cricothyroid muscle. Its close proximity to the superior thyroid artery and the superior pole of the thyroid gland makes it vulnerable during thyroid surgery, especially when ligating the superior thyroid vessels or dissecting the superior pole.
- **Internal Branch of SLN:** Innervates the mucosa of the larynx above the vocal cords, providing sensory innervation. Injury causes loss of sensation in this area, not motor deficits affecting pitch.
- **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN):** Innervates all other intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except cricothyroid), including the posterior cricoarytenoid (abductor), lateral cricoarytenoid, thyroarytenoid, and interarytenoid (adductors). Injury typically causes hoarseness, breathy voice, and potentially airway obstruction depending on the position of the paralyzed vocal cord(s). It runs in the tracheoesophageal groove.
- **Ansa Cervicalis:** A loop of cervical spinal nerves (C1-C3) that innervates the strap muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, omohyoid). Injury affects neck muscle function, not vocal pitch directly.
- **Phrenic Nerve:** Innervates the diaphragm. Injury affects respiration, not vocal function.
- **Conclusion:** The specific deficit of inability to produce high-pitched sounds directly correlates with paralysis of the cricothyroid muscle, which is innervated by the EBSLN. Injury to this nerve during thyroid surgery is a known complication.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve:** This branch provides sensory innervation to the larynx above the vocal folds. Injury would cause loss of sensation (e.g., choking sensation with swallowing, reduced cough reflex) but not paralysis of the cricothyroid muscle or inability to produce high-pitched sounds. Test-takers might confuse it with the SLN but fail to distinguish its sensory function from the motor function of the EBSLN.
- (C) **Recurrent laryngeal nerve:** This nerve innervates all intrinsic laryngeal muscles *except* the cricothyroid. Injury typically causes hoarseness, breathiness, and potential airway compromise due to vocal cord abduction or adduction paralysis. While RLN injury is a common complication of thyroid surgery, it does not specifically cause the inability to produce high-pitched sounds. Test-takers might choose this because it's the most commonly injured nerve during thyroidectomy, but the specific symptom profile points elsewhere.
- (D) **Ansa cervicalis:** This nerve loop innervates the strap muscles of the neck. Injury would cause weakness or paralysis of these muscles (e.g., sternocleidomastoid, sternohyoid, omohyoid), affecting neck movement and potentially respiration, but not vocal pitch control. Test-takers might confuse it with other nerves in the neck region.
- (E) **Phrenic nerve:** This nerve innervates the diaphragm. Injury causes diaphragmatic paralysis, leading to respiratory distress. It is not directly related to laryngeal function or thyroid surgery complications affecting the voice. Test-takers might incorrectly associate it with structures in the neck or chest.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | External Branch SLN (EBSLN) | Internal Branch SLN | Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN) | Ansa Cervicalis | Phrenic Nerve |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------|---------------|
| **Innervation**      | Cricothyroid muscle         | Laryngeal mucosa (above cords) | All other intrinsic laryngeal muscles | Strap muscles | Diaphragm    |
| **Function Affected**| High-pitched voice          | Sensation in larynx  | Hoarseness, breathiness, airway | Neck movement  | Respiration   |
| **Course**           | Near Sup. Thyroid Artery    | With EBSLN initially | Tracheoesophageal groove       | Neck (C1-C3)    | Thorax/Neck  |
| **Thyroidectomy Risk**| High (superior pole)        | Lower                | High (inferior pole, ligament of Berry) | Lower          | Very Low     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Injury to the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve (EBSLN) during thyroid surgery specifically affects the cricothyroid muscle, leading to an inability to produce high-pitched sounds. Differentiating this from RLN injury (hoarseness, breathiness) and internal branch SLN injury (sensory loss) is crucial. Remember the EBSLN's course near the superior thyroid artery makes it vulnerable during superior pole dissection.

<a id='question-086'></a>

### Question 086: Thyroid Surgery Complication
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anatomical relationship between the inferior thyroid artery (a branch of the thyrocervical trunk) and the recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) during thyroid surgery, specifically the risk of RLN injury due to the artery's course. (甲状下动脉与喉返神经在甲状腺手术中的解剖关系及潜在损伤风险)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female undergoes a total thyroidectomy for multinodular goiter. During the dissection of the thyroid gland, the surgeon identifies the superior thyroid artery and ligates it. Subsequently, while carefully dissecting the inferior pole of the thyroid gland near the tracheoesophageal groove, the surgeon encounters a small, pulsating vessel running superiorly towards the thyroid gland. This vessel is closely associated with a nerve that appears to be running parallel to it, heading towards the larynx. Immediately after ligation of this vessel, the patient's voice becomes hoarse and breathy. Postoperative laryngoscopy reveals paralysis of the right vocal cord in the paramedian position.

Which structure was most likely injured during the ligation of the pulsating vessel described?

(A) Superior laryngeal nerve
(B) External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(C) Inferior thyroid artery
(D) Recurrent laryngeal nerve
(E) Tracheoesophageal groove

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old female undergoing total thyroidectomy. This sets the context for potential surgical complications involving structures near the thyroid gland.
- **Procedure:** Total thyroidectomy, specifically dissection at the inferior pole near the tracheoesophageal groove. This is a critical area where the recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) and inferior thyroid artery (ITA) are located.
- **Surgical Finding:** Identification of a "small, pulsating vessel running superiorly towards the thyroid gland" closely associated with a nerve running parallel to it towards the larynx. This strongly suggests the inferior thyroid artery (pulsating vessel) and the recurrent laryngeal nerve (nerve).
- **Complication:** Immediate postoperative hoarseness and breathy voice, confirmed by laryngoscopy showing right vocal cord paralysis in the paramedian position. This is a classic sign of RLN injury, as the RLN innervates all intrinsic laryngeal muscles except the cricothyroid. Paralysis in the paramedian position is typical for RLN injury, whereas a more lateral position might suggest vagus nerve injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure was most likely injured during the ligation of the pulsating vessel. The pulsating vessel is the ITA. The nerve running parallel is the RLN. The complication (vocal cord paralysis) points directly to injury of the RLN. The injury occurred *during* the ligation of the ITA, implying the RLN was either directly injured by the ligation or was closely related and vulnerable during the maneuver.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Inferior Thyroid Artery (ITA):** Arises from the thyrocervical trunk (which itself arises from the subclavian artery). It ascends superiorly, posterior to the carotid sheath, and enters the thyroid gland, typically near the inferior pole. It supplies the inferior portion of the thyroid gland.
- **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN):** A branch of the vagus nerve (CN X). The right RLN loops around the right subclavian artery, while the left RLN loops around the aortic arch. Both ascend in the tracheoesophageal groove, lateral to the esophagus and posterior to the trachea, running deep to the inferior thyroid fascia. The RLN runs *posterior* and *lateral* to the ITA as it ascends towards the larynx. It enters the larynx posterior to the cricothyroid joint.
- **Relationship:** The ITA and RLN are in close proximity in the neck, particularly near the inferior pole of the thyroid gland and within the tracheoesophageal groove. The ITA often crosses *anterior* to the RLN as it ascends towards the thyroid gland. During thyroid surgery, especially at the inferior pole, the ITA must be carefully identified and ligated to control bleeding. Due to their close relationship, the RLN is at significant risk of injury during ITA ligation or dissection in this area.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the RLN during thyroidectomy is a well-known complication, leading to vocal cord paralysis, hoarseness, and potentially airway compromise. The paramedian position of the paralyzed vocal cord is characteristic of RLN injury. The description of the pulsating vessel (ITA) and the nerve (RLN) running together, followed by RLN injury after ligation of the vessel, confirms this anatomical relationship and the risk of injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Superior laryngeal nerve: The superior laryngeal nerve (SLN) branches into an internal (sensory) and external (motor to cricothyroid) branch. The SLN runs superior to the thyroid gland, near the superior pole, and is at risk during ligation of the *superior* thyroid artery. Injury to the SLN typically causes inability to raise the pitch of the voice (due to cricothyroid paralysis) and loss of sensation below the vocal cords. The vignette describes injury occurring at the *inferior* pole and paralysis of the *entire* vocal cord (affecting adduction/abduction), not just pitch control, making SLN injury unlikely.
- (B) External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve: This branch specifically innervates the cricothyroid muscle. Injury causes inability to tense the vocal cords, leading to a monotonous voice and difficulty hitting high notes. As explained for (A), the injury occurred at the inferior pole, and the symptoms (complete vocal cord paralysis) are inconsistent with isolated external branch injury.
- (C) Inferior thyroid artery: While the ITA was the vessel ligated, the question asks which structure was *injured*, leading to the observed complication (vocal cord paralysis). The ITA is an artery; its ligation causes bleeding control, not vocal cord paralysis. The complication described is a *nerve* injury. Although the ITA ligation *caused* the nerve injury due to their proximity, the nerve itself is the structure that was functionally impaired.
- (E) Tracheoesophageal groove: This is an anatomical space, not a structure that can be injured in the way described. The RLN runs *within* the tracheoesophageal groove, but the injury described is a specific nerve injury, not damage to the groove itself. The groove is a landmark, not the injured entity.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location Relative to Thyroid Gland | Innervation/Function                                  | Risk During Thyroidectomy | Clinical Manifestation of Injury                                  |
| :---------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN)** | Inferior pole, tracheoesophageal groove | Motor to all intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except cricothyroid) | High (especially inferior pole) | Hoarseness, breathy voice, vocal cord paralysis (paramedian position) |
| Superior Laryngeal Nerve (SLN) | Superior pole                       | Internal: Sensory to larynx below cords; External: Motor to cricothyroid | Moderate (superior pole) | Monotonous voice, difficulty with high pitch, loss of sensation below cords |
| Inferior Thyroid Artery (ITA)   | Inferior pole                       | Blood supply to inferior thyroid gland                | High (bleeding risk)      | Bleeding (not nerve injury)                                       |
| External Branch of SLN        | Superior pole                       | Motor to cricothyroid muscle                          | Moderate (superior pole) | Inability to tense vocal cords, monotonous voice                 |
| Tracheoesophageal Groove      | Between trachea and esophagus       | Anatomical landmark; contains RLN                     | N/A                      | N/A (it's a space)                                                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The close anatomical relationship between the inferior thyroid artery and the recurrent laryngeal nerve in the tracheoesophageal groove makes the RLN vulnerable during thyroid surgery, particularly when ligating the ITA near the inferior pole. Injury to the RLN results in vocal cord paralysis, typically presenting as hoarseness and a breathy voice with the affected cord in the paramedian position. Differentiating RLN injury from SLN injury is crucial, as SLN injury primarily affects vocal cord tension (pitch) rather than adduction/abduction.

<a id='question-087'></a>

### Question 087: Thyroidectomy Complication: Vocal Cord Paralysis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) innervates all intrinsic laryngeal muscles except the cricothyroid muscle. Injury to the RLN during thyroidectomy can cause vocal cord paralysis, leading to hoarseness (unilateral) or airway obstruction (bilateral). The anatomical course of the RLN, particularly its relationship to the inferior thyroid artery and the thyroid gland itself, makes it vulnerable during surgery. (喉返神经 (RLN) 支配喉部所有内固有肌，除了甲状软骨肌。甲状腺切除术中 RLN 损伤会导致声带麻痹，引起嘶哑 (单侧) 或气道阻塞 (双侧)。RLN 的解剖走行，特别是其与甲状腺下极动脉和甲状腺本身的关系，使其在手术中易受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female undergoes a total thyroidectomy for multinodular goiter. The surgery is uneventful, but postoperatively, the patient complains of a change in her voice, describing it as raspy and weak, especially when speaking for extended periods. Physical examination reveals normal respiratory effort at rest, but forced inspiration against resistance elicits stridor. Laryngoscopy performed 24 hours postoperatively shows the right vocal cord in a paramedian position, while the left vocal cord moves normally. The patient denies shortness of breath at rest.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured during the thyroidectomy, leading to this patient's presentation?

(A) Superior laryngeal nerve
(B) External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(C) Inferior thyroid artery
(D) Cricothyroid muscle
(E) Right recurrent laryngeal nerve

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old female undergoing total thyroidectomy. This sets the context for potential surgical complications involving structures near the thyroid gland.
- **Procedure:** Total thyroidectomy. This procedure involves dissecting around the thyroid gland, putting nearby nerves and vessels at risk.
- **Postoperative Complaint:** Change in voice (raspy, weak). This points towards a problem affecting vocal cord function.
- **Physical Exam:** Normal respiration at rest, but stridor on forced inspiration. Stridor indicates partial airway obstruction, often due to vocal cord dysfunction.
- **Laryngoscopy Findings:** Right vocal cord in a paramedian position, left vocal cord moves normally. This is the crucial finding. A paramedian position of a vocal cord indicates paralysis of the muscles that adduct (close) the vocal cord, specifically the posterior cricoarytenoid (PCA) muscle, which is innervated by the RLN. Unilateral paralysis leads to hoarseness and potential stridor with increased respiratory effort. Bilateral paralysis would lead to severe airway obstruction.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure most likely injured. The findings strongly suggest injury to the nerve supplying the paralyzed vocal cord.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN):** A branch of the vagus nerve (CN X). It loops under the aortic arch (left RLN) or subclavian artery (right RLN) and ascends in the tracheoesophageal groove to enter the larynx.
- **Innervation:** The RLN provides motor innervation to all intrinsic muscles of the larynx *except* the cricothyroid muscle. These muscles include the posterior cricoarytenoid (PCA), lateral cricoarytenoid (LCA), interarytenoid (IA), thyroarytenoid (TA), and vocalis muscles. The PCA is the sole abductor of the vocal cords; paralysis leads to the cord resting in a paramedian position (adducted), causing hoarseness and potential airway compromise.
- **Thyroidectomy Risk:** During thyroidectomy, the RLN is at risk of injury, particularly near the inferior thyroid artery, which often crosses anterior to the nerve. The nerve can be damaged by traction, ligation, or transection.
- **Superior Laryngeal Nerve (SLN):** Also a branch of the vagus nerve. It divides into an internal branch (sensory to the larynx above the vocal cords) and an external branch (motor to the cricothyroid muscle). Injury to the SLN causes inability to tense the vocal cords, leading to a weak, high-pitched voice, but not paralysis or stridor.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's hoarseness and paramedian position of the right vocal cord directly correlate with paralysis of the muscles innervated by the RLN on that side. The right RLN is the most commonly injured during thyroidectomy due to its longer course and closer relationship to the right inferior thyroid artery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior laryngeal nerve:** Injury to the SLN (internal branch) causes loss of sensation above the vocal cords, leading to aspiration risk. Injury to the SLN (external branch) causes paralysis of the cricothyroid muscle, resulting in an inability to tense the vocal cords, leading to a weak, monotonous voice, but *not* vocal cord paralysis in the paramedian position or stridor. This does not match the laryngoscopy findings.
- (B) **External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve:** As explained above, injury affects only the cricothyroid muscle, causing voice changes (weak, high-pitched) but not vocal cord paralysis or stridor. The laryngoscopy shows a paralyzed (paramedian) vocal cord, ruling this out.
- (C) **Inferior thyroid artery:** Injury to the inferior thyroid artery would cause bleeding during or after surgery. While the artery is anatomically close to the RLN and its injury might occur concurrently, damage to the artery itself does not directly cause vocal cord paralysis or hoarseness. The primary cause of the observed symptoms is nerve injury.
- (D) **Cricothyroid muscle:** This muscle is innervated by the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve. Paralysis of this muscle causes inability to tense the vocal cords, leading to a weak, high-pitched voice, but not the paramedian vocal cord position seen in RLN injury.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Innervation                                  | Function Affected                                                                 | Clinical Presentation                                                                 | Laryngoscopy Finding                                  |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN)** | Branch of Vagus (CN X)                      | All intrinsic laryngeal muscles *except* cricothyroid (PCA, LCA, IA, TA, Vocalis) | Hoarseness, stridor (if bilateral or severe unilateral), difficulty swallowing        | Vocal cord in paramedian (adducted) or intermediate position |
| Superior Laryngeal Nerve (SLN) | Branch of Vagus (CN X)                      | Cricothyroid muscle (external branch), larynx above cords (internal branch)       | Weak, high-pitched voice (external branch injury); Aspiration risk (internal branch) | Normal vocal cord position (unless RLN also injured) |
| Cricothyroid Muscle           | External branch of SLN                       | Tenses vocal cords                                                                  | Weak, high-pitched voice                                                              | Normal vocal cord position                             |
| Posterior Cricoarytenoid (PCA) | Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN)              | Abducts (opens) vocal cords                                                         | Stridor, difficulty breathing (if paralyzed in adducted position)                    | Vocal cord in paramedian (adducted) position          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Injury to the recurrent laryngeal nerve during thyroidectomy is a common cause of unilateral vocal cord paralysis, presenting as hoarseness. Bilateral injury leads to airway obstruction (stridor). Differentiating RLN injury (paramedian cord) from SLN external branch injury (normal cord position, weak voice) is crucial. Remember the RLN innervates all intrinsic laryngeal muscles except the cricothyroid.

<a id='question-088'></a>

### Question 088: Left Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The left recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) loops under the aortic arch (specifically, the ligamentum arteriosum, a remnant of the ductus arteriosus) before ascending to innervate the intrinsic muscles of the larynx, making it vulnerable to pathology in the root of the neck and superior mediastinum. (左侧喉返神经在回心神经走行过程中，在主动脉弓下方（具体为动脉韧带，即导管余部）回环，然后向上走行支配喉部内固有肌，使其容易受到颈根部和上纵隔病理的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a 40-pack-year smoking history presents to the emergency department with worsening hoarseness and shortness of breath over the past 3 months. He reports a persistent cough and unintentional weight loss. Computed tomography (CT) scan of the chest reveals a large, saccular aneurysm of the descending thoracic aorta extending into the aortic arch, with significant compression of surrounding structures. During surgical planning for aneurysm repair, the surgeon notes the critical anatomical relationship of the left recurrent laryngeal nerve to the aortic arch. Post-operatively, the patient develops significant dysphonia and aspiration. Laryngoscopy confirms paralysis of the left vocal cord in the paramedian position.

Which structure is most likely compressed by the aortic aneurysm, leading to the patient's post-operative vocal cord paralysis?

(A) Right vagus nerve
(B) Left phrenic nerve
(C) Left superior laryngeal nerve
(D) Left recurrent laryngeal nerve
(E) Ansa cervicalis

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, smoker, hoarseness, shortness of breath, cough, weight loss – suggestive of malignancy or vascular pathology affecting the mediastinum/neck.
- **CT Findings:** Large descending thoracic aortic aneurysm extending into the aortic arch, compressing surrounding structures. This points to a vascular etiology for the symptoms.
- **Post-operative Findings:** Dysphonia (hoarseness) and aspiration, confirmed left vocal cord paralysis in paramedian position. This indicates injury to the nerve supply of the intrinsic laryngeal muscles.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure compressed by the aneurysm causing the vocal cord paralysis.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Left Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN):** A branch of the left vagus nerve (CN X). It arises from the vagus nerve in the thorax, loops *under* the aortic arch (specifically, the ligamentum arteriosum, the remnant of the ductus arteriosus), ascends in the tracheoesophageal groove, and innervates all intrinsic muscles of the larynx except the cricothyroid muscle. Paralysis typically results in vocal cord position at the paramedian position (abduction failure), leading to hoarseness and aspiration.
- **Aortic Arch:** The curved portion of the aorta arising from the left ventricle. The left RLN passes directly beneath its arch.
- **Aortic Aneurysm:** Dilation of the aorta. An aneurysm of the aortic arch or descending aorta can directly compress the left RLN as it loops underneath.
- **Right RLN:** Branches off the right vagus nerve higher in the neck, loops *under* the right subclavian artery, and ascends to the larynx. It is not typically affected by aortic arch pathology.
- **Phrenic Nerve:** Innervates the diaphragm. The left phrenic nerve passes anterior to the aortic arch and root of the left lung; the right passes anterior to the brachiocephalic artery and SVC. While potentially affected by mediastinal masses, it does not innervate the larynx.
- **Superior Laryngeal Nerve:** A branch of the vagus nerve. It divides into internal (sensory to larynx above vocal cords) and external (motor to cricothyroid muscle) branches. Injury causes changes in voice pitch (inability to hit high notes) and loss of sensation above the vocal cords, but not paralysis of the vocal cord itself (unless the RLN is also involved). It runs superiorly, not under the aortic arch.
- **Ansa Cervicalis:** A nerve loop formed by branches of C1-C3 spinal nerves. It innervates the infrahyoid strap muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, omohyoid). It is located in the neck, not near the aortic arch, and does not innervate the larynx.

The clinical presentation (hoarseness, aspiration, left vocal cord paralysis in paramedian position) strongly points to left RLN injury. The CT findings (aortic aneurysm compressing structures) explain the cause. The anatomical course of the left RLN looping under the aortic arch makes it particularly vulnerable to compression by an aortic aneurysm in this location.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Right vagus nerve:** The right vagus nerve gives rise to the right RLN much higher in the neck. Pathology of the aortic arch would not directly affect the right vagus nerve itself, although its branch (right RLN) could be affected by pathology in the neck/upper thorax, but not typically by an aortic arch aneurysm. The symptoms described are specifically left-sided vocal cord paralysis.
- (B) **Left phrenic nerve:** The left phrenic nerve runs anterior to the aortic arch. While it can be compressed by mediastinal pathology, injury causes diaphragmatic paralysis (leading to shortness of breath, especially when lying down), not vocal cord paralysis.
- (C) **Left superior laryngeal nerve:** This nerve innervates the cricothyroid muscle and provides sensation above the vocal cords. Injury causes inability to raise pitch and loss of sensation above the cords, not paralysis of the vocal cord itself (which is innervated by the RLN). Its course is also different, running superiorly, not under the aortic arch.
- (E) **Ansa cervicalis:** This nerve loop innervates the infrahyoid muscles. Injury affects neck muscle function, not laryngeal function. It is located in the neck, far from the aortic arch.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Innervation/Function                                  | Course Relative to Aortic Arch                                  | Clinical Effect of Injury                                                                 | Vulnerability to Aortic Arch Pathology |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- |
| **Left RLN (Correct)**       | Intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except cricothyroid)     | Loops *under* the aortic arch (ligamentum arteriosum)           | Vocal cord paralysis (paramedian position), hoarseness, aspiration                         | **High**                                |
| Right RLN                    | Intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except cricothyroid)     | Loops *under* the right subclavian artery                       | Vocal cord paralysis, hoarseness, aspiration                                              | Low                                     |
| Left Phrenic Nerve           | Diaphragm                                             | Passes anterior to aortic arch                                 | Diaphragmatic paralysis, shortness of breath                                              | Moderate                                |
| Left Superior Laryngeal Nerve | Cricothyroid muscle, sensation above vocal cords       | Runs superiorly in the neck                                    | Inability to raise pitch, loss of sensation above cords                                   | Low                                     |
| Ansa Cervicalis              | Infrahyoid muscles                                     | Located in the neck                                            | Weakness of infrahyoid muscles (e.g., difficulty swallowing)                               | Very Low                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The left recurrent laryngeal nerve's unique course looping under the aortic arch makes it particularly susceptible to compression or injury from pathologies affecting the aortic arch, such as aneurysms, tumors, or enlarged lymph nodes. Differentiating RLN injury from superior laryngeal nerve injury is crucial, as they affect different laryngeal functions. Remember the left RLN loops under the aorta, while the right loops under the subclavian artery.

<a id='question-089'></a>

### Question 089: Right Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve Course
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The right recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) loops under the right subclavian artery, unlike the left RLN which loops under the aortic arch, making it more susceptible to injury in the neck region. (右喉返神经与左喉返神经的走行差异：右侧喉返神经在颈部环绕右锁骨下动脉，而左侧喉返神经环绕主动脉弓，因此右侧喉返神经在颈部更容易受到损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of smoking and heavy alcohol use presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe right-sided neck pain and hoarseness following a recent transjugular central venous line placement. Physical examination reveals significant tenderness over the right supraclavicular fossa. Neurological examination shows paralysis of the right vocal cord, confirmed by laryngoscopy, resulting in a weak, breathy voice and difficulty swallowing (dysphagia). A CT scan of the neck with contrast reveals a hematoma in the right supraclavicular region, compressing the trachea slightly to the left. The patient's vital signs are stable, but he exhibits stridor at rest. Which of the following structures is most likely injured during the central line placement, leading to the observed symptoms?

(A) Ansa cervicalis
(B) Vagus nerve (CN X)
(C) Right phrenic nerve
(D) Right recurrent laryngeal nerve
(E) Right common carotid artery

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, smoker, heavy alcohol use (risk factors for malignancy, but procedure-related injury is the focus).
- **Procedure:** Transjugular central venous line placement. This procedure involves inserting a catheter through the internal jugular vein.
- **Symptoms:** Acute right neck pain, hoarseness, right vocal cord paralysis (confirmed by laryngoscopy), dysphagia, stridor. These symptoms point towards injury to the nerve supply of the larynx.
- **Physical Exam:** Tenderness over the right supraclavicular fossa. This localizes the potential site of injury.
- **Imaging:** CT scan shows a hematoma in the right supraclavicular region. This confirms bleeding and potential compression/injury in that area.
- **Key Clue:** The combination of right vocal cord paralysis, dysphagia, and hoarseness following a procedure involving the right internal jugular vein strongly suggests injury to the right recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN). The RLN innervates most intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except cricothyroid) and provides sensory innervation to the larynx below the vocal folds, contributing to the cough reflex and swallowing. The location of the injury (right supraclavicular fossa) is consistent with the course of the right RLN looping under the right subclavian artery.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Right Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN) Course:** The RLN is a branch of the vagus nerve (CN X). Unlike the left RLN, which loops under the aortic arch in the thorax, the right RLN loops under the right subclavian artery in the neck, typically posterior to the artery and anterior to the clavicle. It then ascends in the tracheoesophageal groove to innervate the intrinsic muscles of the larynx (posterior cricoarytenoid, lateral cricoarytenoid, thyroarytenoid, interarytenoid) and provides sensory innervation below the vocal cords.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the right RLN during procedures in the neck or upper thorax (e.g., thyroidectomy, parathyroidectomy, carotid endarterectomy, central line placement, chest tube insertion) can cause ipsilateral vocal cord paralysis. This results in hoarseness (due to inability to adduct the vocal cord), weak cough, aspiration risk, and sometimes dysphagia. The location of the injury in this case (right supraclavicular fossa) directly corresponds to the path of the right RLN as it loops under the right subclavian artery. The hematoma likely formed due to vessel injury during catheter placement, compressing or directly damaging the nearby RLN.
- **Ansa Cervicalis (A):** A loop of cervical spinal nerves (C1-C3) that innervates the infrahyoid strap muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, omohyoid). Injury would cause weakness in depressing the hyoid bone and larynx, not vocal cord paralysis.
- **Vagus Nerve (CN X) (B):** While the RLN is a branch of the vagus nerve, the vagus nerve itself has multiple functions (parasympathetic innervation to thoracic/abdominal viscera, motor to pharynx/larynx, sensory from viscera/ear). Injury higher up (e.g., in the neck) would cause more widespread deficits, including palate paralysis, pharyngeal weakness, and sensory loss, in addition to RLN symptoms. The specific symptoms point to the terminal branch (RLN) injury in the supraclavicular region.
- **Right Phrenic Nerve (C):** Innervates the right hemidiaphragm. Injury would cause paralysis of the right hemidiaphragm, leading to shortness of breath, especially when lying down. It does not innervate the larynx.
- **Right Common Carotid Artery (E):** A major artery supplying the head and neck. Injury would cause massive hemorrhage and potentially stroke, but not isolated vocal cord paralysis.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The RLN, like all cranial nerves, originates from neural crest cells. The vagus nerve (CN X) arises from the hindbrain. The specific path of the right RLN looping under the right subclavian artery is a key anatomical difference compared to the left RLN, which loops under the aortic arch. This difference is related to the differential growth and positioning of the aortic arches during embryonic development. The right subclavian artery arises from the right 4th aortic arch, while the aortic arch itself is formed primarily from the left 4th arch.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Ansa cervicalis:** Incorrect. This nerve loop innervates the infrahyoid muscles. Injury causes weakness in depressing the hyoid and larynx, not vocal cord paralysis or hoarseness. The location of injury (supraclavicular fossa) is also not typical for ansa cervicalis injury.
- (B) **Vagus nerve (CN X):** Incorrect. While the RLN is a branch of the vagus nerve, the symptoms described (isolated right vocal cord paralysis) are characteristic of injury to the terminal RLN branch, specifically in its course in the neck/supraclavicular region. Injury to the vagus nerve higher up in the neck would typically cause additional deficits like palate paralysis or pharyngeal weakness. The question asks for the *most likely* structure injured given the specific location and symptoms.
- (C) **Right phrenic nerve:** Incorrect. The phrenic nerve innervates the diaphragm. Injury causes diaphragmatic paralysis, leading to respiratory distress. It has no relation to laryngeal function or the supraclavicular region in this context.
- (E) **Right common carotid artery:** Incorrect. Injury to this major artery would cause significant hemorrhage and potentially ischemic stroke, presenting very differently (e.g., massive bleeding, neurological deficits). It does not cause isolated vocal cord paralysis.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Innervation/Function                                   | Location of Injury (Supraclavicular Fossa) | Symptoms of Injury                                                                 | Relationship to Subclavian Artery |
| :---------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Right RLN (Correct)**       | Intrinsic laryngeal muscles (except cricothyroid)     | Loops under right subclavian artery        | Ipsilateral vocal cord paralysis, hoarseness, dysphagia, weak cough, stridor        | Loops *under* the artery          |
| Ansa Cervicalis (Distractor A) | Infrahyoid muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, omohyoid) | Lower neck, anterior triangle             | Weakness depressing hyoid/larynx                                                   | Not related                      |
| Vagus Nerve (Distractor B)    | Multiple (motor, sensory, parasympathetic)            | Neck, thorax, abdomen                     | Wide range of deficits depending on level (palate paralysis, pharyngeal weakness, etc.) | Passes through neck, gives off RLN |
| Right Phrenic Nerve (Distractor C) | Right hemidiaphragm                                  | Neck, thorax                              | Right diaphragmatic paralysis, shortness of breath                                  | Not related                      |
| Right Common Carotid Artery (Distractor E) | Supplies head/neck                               | Neck                                       | Massive hemorrhage, potential stroke                                                | Runs deep in carotid sheath        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The right recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) loops under the right subclavian artery in the neck, making it vulnerable to injury during neck procedures, particularly in the supraclavicular region. This contrasts with the left RLN, which loops under the aortic arch in the thorax. Injury to the RLN causes ipsilateral vocal cord paralysis, leading to hoarseness, dysphagia, and aspiration risk. Differentiating RLN injury from vagus nerve injury requires recognizing the specific deficits and the location of the lesion.

<a id='question-090'></a>

### Question 090: Internal Branch of Superior Laryngeal Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve (SLA) provides sensory innervation to the laryngeal mucosa superior to the vocal folds. Injury to this nerve, often during thyroid surgery or central line placement, results in loss of sensation in this specific region, potentially leading to aspiration. (上喉神经内支提供声带上方的喉黏膜感觉神经支配。该神经损伤，常发生在甲状腺手术或中心静脉置管期间，会导致该区域感觉丧失，可能导致误吸。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female undergoes a total thyroidectomy for papillary thyroid carcinoma. During the procedure, the surgeon inadvertently transects a small nerve branch while ligating the superior thyroid artery near the superior pole of the thyroid gland. Postoperatively, the patient reports a persistent feeling of a foreign body in her throat and difficulty swallowing, particularly liquids, which has led to several episodes of coughing and choking after eating or drinking. Laryngoscopy reveals intact vocal cord movement bilaterally, but the mucosa superior to the vocal folds is unresponsive to tactile stimulation. Sensation below the vocal cords is intact.

Which nerve branch was most likely injured, leading to these specific postoperative sensory deficits?

(A) External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(B) Internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve
(C) Recurrent laryngeal nerve
(D) Vagus nerve (CN X)
(E) Ansa cervicalis

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old female undergoing total thyroidectomy. This context immediately raises suspicion for injury to structures near the thyroid gland, particularly nerves and vessels.
- **Surgical Event:** Transection of a "small nerve branch" while ligating the superior thyroid artery near the superior pole of the thyroid. This pinpoints the anatomical location of the injury. The superior thyroid artery runs alongside the superior laryngeal nerve.
- **Postoperative Symptoms:** Foreign body sensation, dysphagia (especially liquids), coughing/choking (aspiration). These symptoms point towards sensory loss in the pharynx/larynx, impairing the protective gag reflex and potentially affecting swallowing coordination.
- **Laryngoscopy Findings:** Intact vocal cord movement bilaterally rules out significant recurrent laryngeal nerve (RLN) injury (which controls intrinsic laryngeal muscles). Unresponsive mucosa *superior* to the vocal folds indicates loss of sensation in that specific area. Intact sensation *below* the vocal folds further localizes the deficit.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific nerve branch responsible for the sensory loss described.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Superior Laryngeal Nerve (SLA):** A branch of the vagus nerve (CN X). It arises from the inferior vagal ganglion.
- **Branches of SLA:** The SLA divides into two main branches near the superior pole of the thyroid gland:
    - **Internal Branch (Internal SLA):** This is a *purely sensory* nerve. It pierces the thyrohyoid membrane along with the superior laryngeal artery (a branch of the superior thyroid artery). It provides sensory innervation to the laryngeal mucosa *superior* to the vocal folds (supraglottic region), including the epiglottis, aryepiglottic folds, and the laryngeal surface of the base of the tongue. Injury leads to loss of sensation in this area, impairing the cough reflex and potentially causing aspiration, as seen in the patient.
    - **External Branch (External SLA):** This is a *motor* nerve. It innervates the cricothyroid muscle, which tenses the vocal cords and raises pitch. Injury leads to inability to raise pitch and voice fatigue.
- **Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN):** Another branch of the vagus nerve. It provides motor innervation to all intrinsic laryngeal muscles *except* the cricothyroid muscle. It also provides sensory innervation to the laryngeal mucosa *inferior* to the vocal folds (subglottic region) and the trachea. Injury leads to vocal cord paralysis (affecting voice and potentially airway).
- **Vagus Nerve (CN X):** The parent nerve of both SLA and RLN. Injury would cause widespread deficits, including sensory loss in the supraglottic region, motor loss affecting the cricothyroid and all intrinsic laryngeal muscles, and potentially other cranial nerve deficits depending on the level of injury.
- **Ansa Cervicalis:** Formed by branches from C1-C3 spinal nerves. It innervates the infrahyoid muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, omohyoid). Injury would cause weakness in strap muscles of the neck, not laryngeal sensory deficits.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (foreign body sensation, dysphagia, aspiration) and laryngoscopy findings (sensory loss *above* the vocal cords, intact vocal cord movement) are classic for injury to the internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve. The surgical context (thyroidectomy, ligation near superior pole) is consistent with this nerve being at risk.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve:** Incorrect. This branch is motor to the cricothyroid muscle. Injury would cause inability to raise vocal pitch and voice fatigue, not sensory loss above the vocal cords. The patient has intact vocal cord movement, making RLN injury unlikely, but external SLA injury would not cause the described sensory symptoms.
- (C) **Recurrent laryngeal nerve:** Incorrect. This nerve provides motor innervation to most intrinsic laryngeal muscles and sensory innervation *below* the vocal cords. Injury would cause vocal cord paralysis (affecting voice and potentially breathing) and sensory loss *below* the cords. The patient has intact vocal cord movement and sensory loss *above* the cords.
- (D) **Vagus nerve (CN X):** Incorrect. While the SLA is a branch of the vagus nerve, a complete vagus nerve injury would cause much more extensive deficits, including paralysis of the palate, pharynx, larynx (including cricothyroid), and sensory loss in a wider area. The localized sensory loss above the vocal cords with preserved vocal cord movement points specifically to the internal branch of the SLA.
- (E) **Ansa cervicalis:** Incorrect. This nerve innervates the infrahyoid muscles. Injury would cause weakness in neck strap muscles, not laryngeal sensory deficits. It is anatomically distant from the surgical field described.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Internal Branch SLA (Correct Answer) | External Branch SLA | Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN) | Vagus Nerve (CN X) | Ansa Cervicalis |
|-------------------------|--------------------------------------|----------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------|-----------------|
| **Function**            | Sensory (Supraglottic Mucosa)        | Motor (Cricothyroid) | Motor (Intrinsic Larynx except Cricothyroid), Sensory (Subglottic Mucosa) | Motor & Sensory (Pharynx, Larynx, Viscera) | Motor (Infrahyoid Muscles) |
| **Location of Injury**  | Near Superior Thyroid Pole           | Near Superior Thyroid Pole | Tracheoesophageal Groove, Larynx | Skull Base, Neck, Thorax, Abdomen | Neck (Deep)     |
| **Clinical Deficit**    | Loss of Sensation above Vocal Cords, Aspiration Risk | Inability to Raise Pitch, Voice Fatigue | Vocal Cord Paralysis, Sensory Loss below Vocal Cords | Widespread Pharyngeal/Laryngeal/Visceral Deficits | Infrahyoid Weakness |
| **Associated Artery**   | Superior Laryngeal Artery            | Superior Thyroid Artery | Inferior Laryngeal Artery      | N/A                | N/A             |
| **Embryological Origin**| 4th Pharyngeal Arch (Sensory)        | 4th Pharyngeal Arch (Motor) | 6th Pharyngeal Arch (Motor), 4th Arch (Sensory) | Cranial Nerves   | Cervical Nerves |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve provides sensory innervation to the larynx above the vocal cords. Injury, often during thyroid surgery, causes loss of sensation in this area, increasing the risk of aspiration. Differentiate this from the external branch (motor to cricothyroid), the recurrent laryngeal nerve (motor to most intrinsic laryngeal muscles, sensory below cords), and the vagus nerve (parent nerve with broader deficits). Key differentials include RLN injury (vocal cord paralysis) and external SLA injury (pitch change).
